# Introduction.

**Enlightenment** has suffered more through the game that has been played until now with one of our most important words, than those can imagine who use it on every occasion without any ill intent—or rather, without thinking anything at all while doing so.

Among us, the term **Mysteries** refers to the solemnities of our **initiations** <note>original: "Aufnahmen". This refers to the ritual process of admitting a new member into a Masonic lodge.</note>, the ceremonies of our meetings, the hieroglyphs on our **tracing board** <term>Tapis: Originally a carpet (French: tapis) laid on the floor of the lodge, it featured symbolic drawings used to instruct candidates. In modern English Masonry, this is known as the "tracing board."</term>, the workings of our degrees, the secret sciences we are led to expect, the explanations given to us, and those promised to us; in short, almost everything seen, heard, and done in our **sanctuary** <note>original: "Heiligthume". A reverent term for the lodge room or the internal spiritual space of the Order.</note> appears incessantly under this name. There must certainly be a common reason why we assign a common name to all these things, and this reason must undoubtedly be that "something" which we associate with the word **Mysteries**, or at least think—

<summary>The author explores how the term "mysteries" is used to describe nearly every facet of Masonic life—from rituals to symbols—suggesting that such broad application requires a shared underlying definition.</summary>

# Introduction.

might be able to go; but I dare to assert that the usual course of previous investigations has missed the mark. People have taken the preconceived and confused concepts they absorbed from certain **Masonic writings** <term>Masonic writings: refer to the various histories, rulebooks, and philosophical tracts published by Freemasons in the 18th century, which often contained legendary or unverified histories of the Order's origins.</term> and systems as a foundation, mixing up the different types of **ancient mysteries** <note>original: "alten Mysterien." # Introduction.

why we could never emerge from the labyrinth, is to be sought only in the single circumstance that we incessantly continued the construction of this labyrinth.

The **mysteries of the ancients** <note>original: "Mysterien der Alten." These refer to secret religious rites of the Greco-Roman world, such as the Eleusinian Mysteries, which were a major topic of study for 18th-century historians and Freemasons.</note> had a point in time when no **initiate** <term>initiate: an "Eingeweihter," or someone who has been formally admitted into a secret society or religious mystery through a specific ritual and instruction.</term> could mistake their purpose, and consequently could neither neglect them through indifference nor abuse them through **fanaticism** <note>original: "Schwärmerey." A common 18th-century pejorative for irrational religious enthusiasm, mystical delusions, or extremist zeal.</note>. This was the era of their greatest flourishing, which lasted as long as they—as **Warburton** <term>Warburton: William Warburton (1698–1779) was an English bishop and author of "The Divine Legation of Moses." He famously argued that the ancient mysteries were designed to teach the "elite" the truth of monotheism and the soul's immortality, hidden from the common people.</term> has excellently proven—propagated the sublime doctrines of the **unity of God** and the **immortality of the soul**, and in their higher degrees provided partly historical, partly philosophical insights into **popular religion** <note>original: "Volksreligion." This refers to the public, polytheistic myths and rituals of the general population, which the author suggests were explained away as mere allegory or history to the higher-ranking initiates.</note>. Initiation was then anything but an empty, senseless display, which would have left the newly initiated person no wiser. # Introduction.

—to substitute for the ordinary nourishment of this lucrative **disease of the soul** <term>disease of the soul: from the German "Seelenkrankheit," referring here to superstition as a psychological ailment that clouds human judgment.</term>. They eventually took those hated truths with them to the grave, and most of their successors enjoyed the fortune—so desirable to the **idol-priests** <term>idol-priests: from "Götzenpfaffen," a derogatory term for clergy who exploit irrationality or false idols for their own gain.</term> of all eras and peoples—of themselves believing, out of stupidity or **fanaticism** <note>original: "Schwärmerey." In the 18th century, this specifically referred to a kind of irrational religious enthusiasm or "feverish" imagination that Enlightenment thinkers opposed.</note>, what they otherwise would have turned against their own consciences out of self-interest. 

Now the word *mysteries* took on as many different meanings as the prevailing superstition had forms. The single characteristic that all these meanings shared was incomprehensibility, or rather, **irrationality**. The sanctuary, which previously held no secrets for the **initiate** <note>Historically, an initiate was someone who had been formally admitted into the secret knowledge of a cult or society.</note>, now showed him nothing but secrets; and the further he advanced into its inner depths, the more difficult it became for him to make use of his **reason**. The ceremonies increased in number and eccentricity, and the less they offered to the understanding—

# Introduction.

The dazed seeker saw and heard things unlike anything he had encountered in nature, and he deemed them **supernatural**. The **belief in miracles** <term>belief in miracles: from "Wunderglaube," a mindset predisposed to finding divine causes for natural events.</term>, which had grown within him from his earliest childhood, made it easy for the **mystagogue** <note>A mystagogue (from the Greek "mystagogos") was a priest or teacher who initiated others into religious "mysteries," often guiding them through sensory-rich rituals.</note> to lead him to see visible signs of the gods' invisible influence in even the most mundane objects and actions—in washing water and anointing oils, in food and drink, and in the use of these things accompanied by specific spoken formulas. The ancient **hieroglyphs** <note>In this context, the author uses "hieroglyphs" to refer to symbolic images or ritual signs that were once intended to convey rational concepts but became viewed as possessing magical or inherent power.</note>, which previously held a meaning that could be grasped by the mind, now possessed an internal power that had to be accepted through faith. In a word! The entire institution had now become the exact opposite of what it was before, without having significantly changed its outward appearance in the eyes of the world,

# Introduction.

and had lost its old name. *

But even in this state of the ancient mysteries, their objective remained no secret to the **initiates** <term>initiates: from the German "Eingeweihten," referring to individuals who have been formally admitted into the inner circle of a secret society or religious cult through specific rituals.</term>. They were either **deceivers** or the **deceived**; but in both cases, they found what they sought. They had different, yet always specific, ultimate goals of which they were conscious. Some sought health, biological heirs, the forgiveness of sins, invulnerability, oracular pronouncements, secret sciences, magical arts, and whatever else those **treasures of grace** <term>treasures of grace: from "Gnadenschätze," a term for spiritual or supernatural benefits believed to be dispensed by a religious authority or through sacred rituals.</term> might be called, within the sanctuary; while the others extracted money and marks of honor from the former. Both classes knew what they were to think whenever "mysteries" were mentioned.

# Introduction.

No true **Freemason** <term>Freemason: a member of a fraternal organization (the Craft) that traces its origins to the local guilds of stonemasons but evolved into a system of morality and philosophy.</term> would be able to forgive himself if he wasted even a single word on the difference between our **Order** <term>Order: from the German "Orden," referring to the formal organization or fraternity of Freemasonry.</term> and the mysteries just described. Yet it is this very enormous difference that seems to put it beyond all doubt that we have even less to expect from the ancient mysteries in their decline than in their prime regarding enlightenment on the subject of our own—however useful the sad history of their degeneration might be in warning against real and possible abuses of Freemasonry. # Introduction.

From the solemn declaration of the highest leaders of our **Order** * we know that our purpose is **benevolence** <term>benevolence: translated from "Wohlthätigkeit," this refers to active goodwill and humanitarian efforts, considered a cornerstone of Masonic practice.</term> in the most extensive sense; consequently, this purpose is by no means the secret whose concealment we have signed with the great oath. Just as little as our hieroglyphs <term>hieroglyphs: in this context, the symbolic figures, emblems, and allegorical images used in Masonic rituals and decorations.</term> can constitute our entire secret, they certainly belong to this secret, because they are included in our oath and would be meaningless without reference to it. # Introduction.

—glyphs themselves. They alone still continue to speak, while our masters have fallen silent; they are the most secure custodians, the most reliable interpreters, and the most incorruptible witnesses to the intentions of our ancestors. It is not without reason that we are so emphatically urged by the **Order** itself to study them diligently.

Based on this premise, treatises on the mysteries of the ancients should by no means begin with the collection and presentation of scattered fragments of their history, only to end by searching for accidental similarities to **Masonry**. Instead, they should proceed from our **sanctuary** <term>sanctuary: translated from "Heiligthum," this refers to the lodge or the inner sacred space where Masonic rituals are performed and symbols are preserved.</term>, attaching the thread of investigation to our most excellent and meaningful **hieroglyphs** <term>hieroglyphs: as noted previously, these are the symbolic emblems and allegorical images of the Masonic tradition.</term>. This thread should then be followed through the history of their literal meaning, their original use, and their various fates, tracing them back to their very origin.

# Introduction.
B

...then return once more to our **sanctuary** <term>sanctuary: translated from "Heiligthum," referring to the lodge or inner sacred space where rituals are performed</term>, in order to learn, through a precise comparison of the individual **hieroglyphs** <term>hieroglyphs: the symbolic emblems and allegorical images of the Masonic tradition</term> with one another, their collective purpose—or at least the spirit of their original composition. The interior of the **sanctuary**, which until now lay before his eyes shrouded in the densest darkness, will admittedly only be illuminated for him by individual flashes of insight; but if he sees nothing thereby, he may always suspect his own eyes.

# Introduction.

Finally, no people can display as many hieroglyphs, ceremonies, and ritual laws as the **Hebrews**; at the very least, we possess records for no other people that are so precise and detailed. ### First Section.

an abomination to them. Indisputably, the contempt and hatred under whose pressure the Hebrews grew into a nation were not only a consequence, but also a contributing cause of that inhumanity found in their dull and heartless character—a point upon which Holy Scripture is in such perfect agreement with secular history <note>The author uses "profane history" to refer to non-religious or academic historical accounts of antiquity.</note>, and of which, to the honor of humanity, one can find no example in any other people. They were transformed from Canaanite herdsmen into Egyptian slaves, and were treated by their masters far worse than the Helots <note>The Helots were a subjugated population in ancient Sparta, legally tied to the land and often subjected to ritualized mistreatment.</note> ever were by the Spartans. If it were true, as the pagan writers have accused them, that the Egyptians had found themselves forced to drive them out of the land because of their infectious leprosy <note>original: "Aussatz." In ancient contexts, this term often covered various skin diseases viewed as marks of impurity.</note> and their robberies, it would have done the Egyptians little credit that they felt compelled to punish their servants so harshly for the necessary consequences of their own cruelty.

This ancient Egyptian hatred has been inherited by later writers of this nation, as well as those of all other nations in the world. Certainly, the singular agreement of pagan historians regarding the circumstances of the expulsion of the Hebrews from Egypt is a consequence of this.

### First Section.

However far the mutual loathing between the **Egyptians** and **Israelites** may have gone, it could not prevent the latter from adopting much of the customs, habits, and ways of thinking of a land where they and their ancestors had been born and raised for several generations, and whose laws they were forced to follow for four centuries. 

Who does not know from their Bible how much time and effort it cost before they broke themselves of even their crudest Egyptian bad habits? The **idols** and **fleshpots** <term>fleshpots: translated from 'Fleischtöpfe,' a biblical reference to Exodus 16:3, where the Israelites remembered the meat-filled cooking pots of Egypt with longing during their hunger in the wilderness</term> of that land remained in fond memory among them for many years, and their frequent attempts to return to both are unambiguous proof of an old habit that had become second nature. In a word! Among the **Hebrews** who came out of Egypt, everything was Egyptian, from the gold and silver vessels which they secretly took with them on their journey—to the wisdom of their leader and lawgiver <note>A reference to Moses; the author suggests that even the foundational laws of Israel were shaped by Moses's Egyptian education.</note>.

# Of the Hebrews in Egypt.
->C<-

We have in Saint Stephen in the Acts of the Apostles <note>Referencing Acts 7:22: "And Moses was learned in all the wisdom of the Egyptians."</note> an irreproachable witness that Moses was instructed in all the wisdom of the Egyptians, and Philo <note>Philo of Alexandria (c. 20 BCE – 50 CE), a Hellenistic Jewish philosopher who sought to harmonize Jewish scripture with Greek philosophy.</note> explains himself even more clearly on this by asserting that, "Moses was initiated by the Egyptian priests into the philosophy of symbols and hieroglyphs, as well as into the secrets of the sacred animals." This testimony, confirmed by the Church Fathers and other writers, and the circumstance that Moses was adopted as a child ** by the daughter of the Pharaoh of that time—and consequently must have enjoyed all the privileges of an Egyptian of the highest rank—could not allow us to doubt for a single moment his initiation into the mysteries of the land, even if we [were to consider] the many mark <note>The text breaks off mid-word; "Merks" is likely the start of "Merkmale," meaning "characteristics" or "signs."</note>

\* [He learned] the philosophy delivered through symbols, which they describe with letters they call sacred, and that which pertains to the reception of animals which are venerated with divine honors. # On the Hebrews in Egypt.

Moses, who appears as *Mysi* in Ausonius* and as *Musäus* in Artapanus**, and even by name seems not very different from our Moses or Moyses—was, as is well known, saved in his childhood like the Hebrew leader, and subsequently likewise became, like this benefactor, a legislator and prince of his people. Like him, he was deemed worthy of intimate communion with a deity, and marched with an almost equally numerous army through the Red Sea <note>The author is conflating the crossing of the Red Sea with mythological accounts of Bacchus crossing the waters of the East during his legendary Indian campaign.</note> on dry feet. To him also is attributed a miraculous rod <term>rod: a staff or wand used to perform miracles, common in ancient depictions of magicians and prophets</term>, with which he forced the rivers Orontes and Hydaspes <note>The Orontes is in modern-day Syria; the Hydaspes is the Jhelum River in India. These geographical spans reflect the vast conquests attributed to the god Bacchus.</note> to give way before his people, and which he could transform into a serpent at his will. Finally, to complete the abundance of similarities, he did not even lack the golden horn, or the rays upon his forehead, with which Moses [descended] from Sinai

### Of the Hebrews in Egypt.

... [the respect I] owe to the Mosaic worship <term>Mosaic worship: the religious laws and rituals established by Moses, traditionally called the "Gottesdienst" or service to God</term>, by no means to infringe upon it, when I venture to call this worship in its essential components a faithful copy of the secret religion of the Egyptians, and to assert that the legislator of the Hebrews <note>Moses, whom the author views as an Egyptian-trained statesman</note>, by all appearances, intended to initiate his entire people, as far as was possible, into the Egyptian mysteries <term>mysteries: secret religious rites of the ancient world, such as those at Eleusis or Memphis, which were reserved for initiates rather than the general public</term>.

In the proofs I have to present regarding this, it depends mainly on the following two points: First, that the legislator made the object—or what is the same thing, the secret doctrine <term>secret doctrine: the "Geheimlehre," or the esoteric truths about the unity of God that were supposedly hidden from the common people in Egypt</term> of the mysteries—not only the foundation but also the public fundamental doctrine of the Hebrew religion; and second, that he borrowed the most distinguished ceremonies and hieroglyphs of his new worship from those mysteries, and retained them with as few changes as were necessary for his purpose. I hope to bring these two main propositions to the highest degree of probability in the next sections.

## Second Section.

### On the Subject of the Lesser Mysteries of the Hebrews.

I take it as fully proven—based on Warburton’s* famous work on the divine mission of Moses and many Masonic treatises—that the highest object of the ancient mysteries in their peak state, or the secrets of the Epopteia <note>original Greek: "ΕΠΟΠΤΕΙΑ." This refers to the highest stage of initiation in the Greek mysteries, where the initiate became a "beholder" of sacred truths</note>, consisted firstly of the doctrine of the unity of God, and secondly of a historical-philosophical refutation of the errors in common folk religion, or polytheism <note>original: "Vielgötterey"</note>. With even greater certainty, beyond all contradiction, I can assume that these two secret objects of the mysteries were simultaneously the fundamental teachings of the new Mosaic religion. This inner and essential kinship of Hebraism <note>original: "Hebraismus." Referring to the religious and cultural system of the ancient Hebrew people</note> with the mysteries is so evident that it is as little as—

"To the Hebrew people alone," are his words, "was the honor reserved to be **initiated** into the knowledge of the Creator of all things." The words <term>epopteia<note>original: "ἐποπτεία" — the highest stage of initiation, involving the 'beholding' or direct vision of the mysteries.</note></term> (the beholding of the secrets), <term>theoria<note>original: "θεωρία" — the contemplation or study of the divine.</note></term> (the contemplation of them), and <term>Demiurge<note>original: "Δημιουργὸς" — literally "craftsman," a term used in Greek philosophy and the mysteries to describe the Creator as the object of their knowledge.</note></term> (the Creator as their object), which appear in the Greek text, are clearly expressions that were originally used only in the <term>greater mysteries<note>The most sacred and secret parts of ancient religious festivals, where the deepest "truths" were revealed.</note></term>.

they did not even know **Jehovah** by name.\* Their thoughtless minds found it so difficult to grasp and hold onto the idea of a single God that they discarded it at the slightest provocation, and even believed they had to thank the gods of that land for their astonishing liberation from Egypt with a festival of joy.\*\*

Despite the readiness of this superstitious people to multiply the objects of their devotion, **Moses** would have found it even more difficult to gain acceptance for his **Jehovah** among his countrymen if he had not announced Him under the name of the God of their fathers. This designation played into the generally accepted folk beliefs of those times, according to which every nation had to have its own special protective god; and one could expect nothing less from the pride of the Israelites than that they did not wish to be inferior to other nations, who had their **Osiris** <note>The Egyptian god of the afterlife and fertility.</note>, **Chemosh** <note>The national god of the Moabites.</note>, **Milcoms** <note>The primary deity of the Ammonites.</note>, **Baals** <note>A title meaning "Lord," used for various local Canaanite deities.</note>, and **Ashtaroths** <note>The plural form of Ashtoreth, a Phoenician and Canaanite goddess of fertility and war.</note>, etc.

And now, my brothers! I ask you to observe well the important circumstance that the name which Moses presented to them in response to this question was completely unknown not only to the Israelites living then, but also to their ancestors, and even to the patriarchs Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob. We learn this fact from God himself, who expressly said to Moses: <margin>* 2nd Book of Moses <note>Exodus</note> Chapter 6, Verse 3.</margin> * “I appeared to Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob under the name of the Almighty, but my name Jehovah was not revealed to them.” — The Israelites had therefore by no means brought the name Jehovah with them to Egypt; rather, they heard it for the first time shortly before their departure from that land, and Moses was the first who made them acquainted with it.

“The most sacred name among the Egyptians,” writes Voltaire <note>François-Marie Arouet (1694–1778), the famous French Enlightenment philosopher.</note> in his *Essay on the Customs and the Spirit of Nations*, “was the very same one that the Hebrews later adopted into their language, namely J—ha—ho,” or as he writes it in other places according to Clement of Alexandria <note>An early Christian theologian (c. 150–215 AD) who studied Egyptian traditions.</note>: J—a—o. “One

## Second Section.

“...pronounced this word in various ways, and the aforementioned Clement <note>Clement of Alexandria, a 2nd-century theologian</note> asserts in his *Stromata* <note>original: *Stromaten*; a series of books titled "Miscellanies" or "Patchwork" exploring Greek philosophy and Christian faith</note>, that no one was allowed to enter the temple of Serapis <note>A Graeco-Egyptian god whose cult combined Egyptian and Greek features</note> who did not wear the name J—ha—ho, or Jao, which designates the eternal God, on his breast or forehead. When we consider how much the pronunciation of a word must change over a period of millennia, the difference between the words J—ha—ho and Jehovah, given the remaining similarity of their construction and meaning, should not prevent us from regarding them as originally one and the same symbol of thought.”

However, this Voltairean observation may be nothing more than a hint, which I gladly leave to the examination of insightful brothers <note>Likely referring to the "Brethren" of a Masonic or secret society</note>. I believe I have stronger reasons for the mystical origin of this name.

**Jehovah** means, according to its Hebrew etymology, as much as: **He who is**, and signifies self-existence, the so-called **essence of the Godhead**, the attribute from which all others can be derived, the most sublime of all divine qualities, which was revealed to the **Epopten** <term>Epopten: From the Greek *epoptes*, meaning "one who has seen." seemed in some way to presuppose a plurality of gods, as is evident, among other things, from a passage by Trismegistus <note>Hermes Trismegistus: A legendary figure representing a blend of the Greek god Hermes and the Egyptian god Thoth, credited with ancient mystical writings.</note> cited by Lactantius <note>Lactantius (c. 250–325 AD): An early Christian author who often used pagan prophecies to support Christian doctrines.</note>.*

The region around Mount Sinai was the place where the solemn initiation <term>initiation: The German *Einweihung* here implies a ritual induction into a sacred mystery, framing the biblical covenant as a formal rite of passage.</term> of the Israelites as the People of God took place, after they had survived the heavy trials of their long and dangerous journey. Here they were questioned as to whether they wished to keep the new covenant of the Lord; and once all the people had answered and pledged absolute obedience, the revelation of **Jehovah** was announced to them for the third day.

Now the preparations for their consecration began. They were required to wash their clothes and to abstain from all enjoyment of sensual love. <note>original: *sinnlichen Liebe*. This refers to the biblical command in Exodus 19:15 for the Israelites to remain celibate for three days in preparation for the divine encounter.</note> Barriers were erected around the mountain where the cloud of **Jehovah** was to appear, which no one was permitted to approach under penalty of death.

"At last, on the third day," says the holy text, "there arose thundering and lightning, and a thick cloud upon the mountain, and the sound of very loud trumpets. <note>original: *Posaunen*. While translated as trumpets, in a biblical context, this refers to the *shofar* or ram's horn.</note> But all the people who were in the camp were terrified." ### Section Three.

### Concerning the Hieroglyphs and Ritual Laws of the Lesser Mysteries of the Ancient Hebrews.

In the days of the general captivity of our reason under the yoke of faith, our theologians were undecided whether they should seek the ultimate causes of the Mosaic ritual laws in the wisdom, or rather in the arbitrary will, of God. The latter occurred whenever they were abandoned by their own wisdom in the investigation of these final causes. For alas! It was not possible with every Jewish ceremony to easily turn it into a prophetic symbol <term>Sinnbilde: an image or emblem intended to convey a deeper moral or religious meaning</term> of a scholastic whim or a monkish formula of faith. Moreover, they were in complete agreement among themselves that all these ceremonies—from the mutilation of the foreskin <note>A reference to circumcision.</note> to the trial of a woman's chastity with the water that causes the belly to swell*—were invented directly by God himself, and that just like every line of the entire Scripture, the Hagio- <note>original: *Hagio-*. This is a catchword, an old printing practice where the first syllable of the next page is printed at the bottom of the current one to ensure the pages were bound in the correct order. It likely leads to "Hagiographers" (holy writers).</note>

---

* Numbers, Chapter 5, Verses 12–31. <note>original: *4. Buch Mosis* (The Fourth Book of Moses), which is the traditional German title for the Book of Numbers.</note>

<summary>This page critiques earlier theological views that attributed the complex and often strange ritual laws of the Old Testament to God's arbitrary will rather than exploring their symbolic or historical origins.</summary>

graphers <note>This completes the word "Hagiographen" from the previous page.</note> had been dictated by the pen [of God]. The latter divides the Hebrew ritual laws into two classes. The first of these contains those that were prompted by the religious customs of the <term>Sabaeans</term> <note>An ancient South Arabian people; Maimonides used the term to refer to ancient pagans or idolaters in general.</note>. They were set in opposition to the manners and customs of these profane nations and had the political intention of keeping the Israelites distant from all communion with them. Included here are also all those ritual laws that had no other purpose than to safeguard the Israelites against a relapse into <term>idolatry</term> <note>The worship of idols or false gods, seen as the primary threat to monotheism.</note>. ### Third Section

...others to call the former a copy of the latter.

<term>Clement of Alexandria</term> <note>An early Christian theologian (c. 150–215 AD) who sought to reconcile Greek philosophy with Christian faith.</note>, to whom we are indebted for so much information regarding the ancient mysteries, asserts that among the other essential conditions for admission into the inner sanctuary, circumcision was also required. At the very least, this was the case during the time of <term>Pythagoras</term> <note>The famous Greek philosopher and mathematician who traveled extensively in Egypt to study their wisdom.</note>; for this philosopher found himself compelled to allow that unphilosophical ceremony to be performed upon him, as he could not have satisfied his curiosity for any lower price. 

According to the report of <term>Epiphanius</term> <note>Epiphanius of Salamis (c. 310–403 AD), a bishop known for his "Panarion," a massive work cataloging and refuting various heresies.</note>, circumcision was initially an exclusive privilege of the priests and <term>mystagogues</term> <note>The individuals who initiated others into the sacred mysteries.</note>; however, at the time the Hebrews left Egypt, it was—according to the testimony of <term>Origen</term> <note>A prolific early Christian scholar (c. 184–253 AD) from Alexandria.</note>—not only a general requirement but also a preliminary ceremony for every initiation. In this context, just as it did later among the Jews, it signified a distinction and a privilege.

\*\*\* Origen, *Homily 5 on Jeremiah* <note>original: *Homilia 5 in Ieremiam*.</note>

### Third Section.

...whether it contained hieroglyphs or sacred implements, I do not venture to decide. Once the spirit of this institution had vanished along with its rational purpose, the <term>hierophants</term> <note>High priests who interpreted sacred mysteries and displayed holy objects to the initiated.</note>—who wished to preserve the external structure for their own ends—had no choice but to turn the very lack of secrets (which seekers expected to find among them) into a secret itself. The locked chest, which might have once served as a <term>symbol</term> <note>original German: *Sinnbild*</note> of hidden truths invisible to the spiritual eyes of the uninitiated, now truly concealed the deception of the hierophants; it hid those very truths which, if known, would have saved the initiates their money, their time, and their sanity. ## V. On Hieroglyphs and Ritual Laws, etc.

now became one of the most prominent machines in the entire speculation of the holy <term>mystery-mongers</term> <note>original German: *Geheimnißkrämer* — a derogatory term for those who profit from making things seem more mysterious than they are.</note>. Through it, the curiosity of the deceived was kept in a state of perpetual suspense; its contents occupied their heated imaginations with countless conjectures, and the <term>Mystagogues</term> <note>Priests who initiated others into the sacred mysteries.</note> did not fail to provide those mysterious hints whose magical power their successors in this trade still know how to use so well even today. For that which is easy to understand or to prepare, the COMMON PEOPLE despise: for they have need of portents and superstition. And why not? Being the common people as they are! — Synesius in his *Praise of Baldness* <note>original Latin: *Calvitiae Encomium*.</note>.

# Third Section.

...held hieroglyphs or sacred implements, I do not dare to decide. It is enough that almost all the reports remaining to us from the mysteries of the ancients speak of mysterious chests, which played one of the most important roles during the ceremonies.

This applies particularly to the periods of the decline of the mysteries. After the rational purpose of this institution had vanished along with its spirit, the <term>Hierophants</term> <note>High priests who "showed" or interpreted sacred symbols; the term comes from the Greek *hieros* (holy) and *phainein* (to show).</note>, who wished to maintain the external form for their own ends, had no other means left but to turn the very lack of the secrets—which people sought from them—into a secret itself. The locked chest, which formerly might have been the <term>symbol</term> <note>original German: *Sinnbild* — a physical object representing a deeper, abstract truth.</note> of hidden truths invisible to uninitiated spiritual eyes, now truly concealed the fraud of the Hierophants; and with it, those very truths that would have saved the initiates their money, their time, and their reason. It

> [would] be; but among the Hebrews, <term>Moloch</term> <note>A name used in the Hebrew Bible for a deity or a type of sacrifice; the author here identifies him with solar worship.</note> signified the sun, which according to their Egyptian folk belief was the residence of the soul of King <term>Osiris</term> <note>The Egyptian god of the afterlife and rebirth, often associated with the sun or the Nile.</note> (your king).

<header>V. On Hieroglyphs and Ritual Laws, etc.</header>

[The chest] now became one of the most prominent <term>engines</term> <note>original German: *Maschinen*—here referring to a device or theatrical mechanism used to create a specific effect on an audience.</note> in the entire speculation of the holy <term>mystery-mongers</term> <note>original: *Geheimnißkrämer*—a pejorative term for those who make a business out of secrecy.</note>. Through it, the curiosity of those being deceived was kept in a state of constant tension; its contents occupied their heated imaginations with countless conjectures, and the <term>Mystagogues</term> <note>Initiators into the mysteries; those who lead the way into sacred rites.</note> did not fail to provide those mysterious hints whose magical power their successors in this trade still know how to use so well today. To this category belong the mystical chests in which, according to the testimony of <term>Synesius</term>* <note>Synesius of Cyrene (c. 373–414 AD), a philosopher and bishop whose works bridged the gap between Neo-Platonism and Christianity.</note>, the Egyptian initiators carefully hid all sorts of <term>spheres</term> <note>Likely celestial models or globes representing the movements of the stars, which were central to ancient Egyptian theology.</note> and magical instruments from the sight of their pupils. Regarding this, the writer expressed himself as follows: “If the common man were to see them, he would take it poorly; for everything that is easy to understand and to do is despised by this class of people, who always scent only miracles and superstitious stuff.”

---

<margin>*</margin> They have *komastēria* <note>original: κομαστήρια—Greek for festive processions or the specialized chests carried during them.</note> which are chests containing, as they say, certain hidden spheres; if the common people were to see these, they would be annoyed. For that which is easy to understand or to prepare, the COMMON PEOPLE despise: for they have need of portents and superstition. And why not? Since they are the common people! — Synesius in *In Praise of Baldness*. <note>original Latin: *Habent κομαστήρια quae arcae sunt, quasdam... Synesius in Calvitia Encomio.*</note>

Much more important than even the **Ark of the Covenant** itself were the **Cherubim** <note>Angelic beings often depicted with wings, representing the presence and majesty of God.</note> placed upon its cover for the religion and divine service of the **Hebrews**. ### Section Three.

Cherubim, and sits between the Cherubim, \* and the **Israelites** were required to direct their faces and their hands toward these images during their prayers and sacrifices.

Whether one considers the **Cherubim** by their name, their form, and their placement, or according to their significance and their final purpose, there is a remarkable agreement among all the circumstances necessary to prove that they were originally **hieroglyphs** of the **Egyptian mysteries** <note>The secret religious rites and symbolic wisdom of ancient Egypt, which many Enlightenment-era scholars believed influenced early Hebrew laws.</note>.

The word **Cherub** signifies a **bull** in the original language and was—as **Spencer** <note>John Spencer (1630–1693), a pioneering English scholar of Hebraic studies who argued that many Jewish rituals were "accommodated" from Egyptian customs.</note> excellently demonstrates—applied to the animal figures on the **Ark of the Covenant** because they resembled the form of a bull. Among all ancient nations, the bull was a symbol of power and strength; in the peak of the **Egyptian** mysteries, the **Apis** <note>The sacred bull of Memphis, worshipped as a deity and seen as a symbol of fertility and divine power.</note> specifically represented the divine attribute of **omnipotence**. Now, since the Ark of the Covenant, upon which the

</margin>

<header>V. Concerning Hieroglyphs and Ritual Laws, etc.</header>

Cherubim rested, and is called at times the Ark of the Lord's strength, \* at other times the sanctuary of power, \*\* and sometimes the strength of the power of the Lord, \*\*\* then the Egyptian sense in which the word Cherub was originally used here is surely beyond any doubt.

The form of the Cherubim was clearly hieroglyphic. It was a composition of various animal forms; and as much as can be gathered from the vision of Ezekiel,† from the reports of Josephus,†† and the testimony of the Church Fathers, they had the head of a human, the back and mane of a lion, the thighs and feet of a bull, and four eagle wings. This custom of uniting several animal forms into one and the same statue undoubtedly first arose in Egypt; and even if it was not initiated by the hierophants <note>Ancient Greek term for priests who interpreted sacred mysteries and esoteric symbols, here used to refer to Egyptian priestly scholars.</note>,

### V. Of Hieroglyphs and Ritual Laws, etc.

Finally, compare the following words of **Josephus**<margin>*</margin> with this: “The Cherubim are winged creatures, which are similar to none of all those seen among men.” — I shall skip a multitude of passages from ancient writers who say roughly the same thing, and merely remind you of the mysterious **Sphinxes** of the Egyptians, in which at least the heads and manes of the Cherubim are unmistakable.

### Section Three.

...I wish to place here from the Talmud. <margin>* From Valentin Schindler, *Lexicon Pentaglotton*, Column 267. A. <note>A 17th-century multilingual dictionary of Hebrew, Chaldean, Syriac, Talmudic-Rabbinic, and Arabic.</note></margin> "The four animal forms in the Cherub represent the four mighty ones of the earth: the lion among the wild animals, the bull among the tame, the eagle among the birds, and man among all living creatures; but God, who sits enthroned upon the Cherubim, is the most powerful being of all." The animal images of the Mysteries <note>Secret religious rites of the ancient world, such as the Isiac or Eleusinian mysteries, which used symbols to convey hidden truths.</note> had a similar meaning. Nothing is more certain than that every **hieroglyph** <note>In this context, the author uses "hieroglyph" to mean any symbolic sacred image, not just Egyptian writing.</note> in the golden age of this institution had its rational meaning.

The **Cherubim** occupied the most prominent place in the Hebrew sanctuary. Whether and to what extent I was justified, moreover, in counting the Jewish religion among the ancient mysteries and calling the Hebrews a nation of initiates, I must leave entirely to the judgment of my brothers <note>The author is addressing fellow Freemasons.</note>. I believe I have shown thus far that the Hebrews were, firstly, in possession of the truths which were either not present at all among other nations or existed only under the veil of secrecy and formed the main object of all genuine mysteries; and secondly, that they had several mystical initiations, hieroglyphs, and ceremonies. whoever is accustomed to understanding this name in the strictest sense; who presupposes in it a fund of insights to which only a small number of people are receptive; hieroglyphs <note>In 18th-century Masonic discourse, "hieroglyphs" referred to symbolic images, emblems, or allegorical signs used to convey moral and philosophical truths to members.</note> that can become equally understandable to every initiate; an initiation with which all secrets cease; secrets that cannot be explained just as well outside of the sanctuary <note>The "sanctuary" refers to the inner, sacred space of a temple or, metaphorically, the private assembly of a Masonic Lodge.</note>; in short, whoever is so exacting regarding the mysteries, to him I can certainly offer no objection if he reproaches me, saying that I have poorly applied this word to the common folk-religion of the Hebrews, and have made myself guilty of the very abuse about which I complained in the introduction. 

However, by this very objection, he then binds himself to renounce this very word whenever common Freemasonry in its current state is being discussed. I, on the other hand, do not yet give up my assertion of Hebrew mysteries for that reason: for perhaps within the heart of Hebraism <note>The religious system, laws, and cultural framework of the Hebrew people.</note>, just as in the heart of Freemasonry, there are secrets to which this designation belongs in the most literal sense.

# V. On the Greater Mysteries of the Ancients in General.

...to let themselves be ruled by the immortal gods themselves.

The confidants and favorites of the gods were thus the first peaceful possessors of sovereign power <term>sovereign power</term><note>obrigkeitliche Gewalt: The authority or legal power exercised by a ruler or governing body over a people.</note> among men. Both their office and their right to it were considered divine, and the belief in their divinity, which was eventually transferred to their very persons, persisted so far beyond the first steps of civilization that the folk religion <term>folk religion</term><note>Volksreligion: The collective religious beliefs and practices of a common people, which in antiquity often centered on local myths and ancestral heroes.</note> of the most enlightened nations could point to no other deities than the first princes of their ancestors. Even the later kings, who did not receive the honor of full deification <term>deification</term><note>Vergötterung: Also known as apotheosis; the act of elevating a human to the status of a god.</note>, were commonly honored in Homer <note>Homer: The legendary Greek poet, author of the Iliad and the Odyssey, whose works often portray kings as "Zeus-nourished" or divine in origin.</note> and other poets with the epithets of * **sons of gods** and **pupils of the gods**.

Theocracy <term>Theocracy</term><note>Theokratie: A form of government in which a deity is recognized as the supreme ruling authority, usually administered by a priestly class or "divinely inspired" kings.</note> is, after the patriarchal form, the oldest of all known forms of government; and one could not cite any ancient nation whose state constitution <term>state constitution</term><note>Staatsverfassung: The fundamental organizational framework and laws of a political community.</note> was not, at least in its origin, theocratic. ### V. On the Greater Mysteries of the Ancients in General.

...whatever one might think of the divine missions of all these prophets and legislators, one must at least be convinced by one's own Bible that, with the sole exception of the Mosaic mission, all others were based on nothing but the superstition of the masses <note>original: "großen Haufens" – literally the "great heap," a common 18th-century term for the uneducated populace.</note> and the cunning of their leaders.

We need know no more than what has been cited thus far to recognize the necessity of political-theological secrets. These had to be as old as the most ancient state constitutions—secrets upon which the prestige of the legislators and their laws was founded, and to which no one but the legislators and their assistants were permitted to hold the key. The possessors could not take this key with them to the grave if they wished to preserve their great work through the same means by which it had been begun. They were thus obliged to leave it behind for their successors or—which amounts to the same thing—to establish Mysteries <term>Mysteries</term><note>Secret religious and political rites, such as the Eleusinian or Egyptian Mysteries, where initiates were taught "higher truths" hidden from the general public.</note> alongside the new state constitution.

Egypt, the school of legislators, was also notoriously the father-

# First Section.

land of the Mysteries. From here, they were spread through Greece and Asia, and the diversity of the gods whose names they adopted in different countries is merely further proof that the founders, when introducing them, were not so much concerned with Egyptian theology as with the use of the **political artifice** <term>political artifice</term><note>The author suggests that the "Mysteries" were not truly about religion, but were a clever "trick" or strategy used by rulers to maintain social order.</note> that they had learned in the sanctuary of this people.

Even after civil constitutions had already taken firm root and secular power began to separate from spiritual power, these Mysteries continued to exist here and there in their original and essential content. As this institution could not possibly remain invisible and hidden amidst the progress of culture, it either had to cease to exist or take on an external form that would lead the **masses** <note>original: "Pöbel" – a term used in the 18th century to describe the uneducated common people.</note> to suspect anything else in the world rather than its internal reality. And what exterior was more suitable for this, and more appropriate to the founders' primary purpose, than one through which the popular religion would gain from the common man everything that it, in the sanctuary itself, [gained] from the initi- <note>The text cuts off mid-word; "Eingeweihten" likely completes the sentence as "initiates."</note>

### Concerning the Greater Mysteries of the Ancients in General.

[...initi-]ates <note>Completing the word "Eingeweihten" from the previous page.</note> had to lose. Thus, Lesser and Greater Mysteries arose; the one concealed what the other revealed; the one tied the knot that the other untied.

There were, therefore, Lesser Mysteries, which were granted to every pious believer without distinction, and which were sometimes withheld only with the intention of increasing the **masses'** <note>original: "großen Hauffens" — referring to the uneducated public.</note> opinion of their value. Their form was adapted to the various needs of different lands, the character of the people, or much more to the nature of the prevailing superstition; they consisted of ceremonies and pageantry that had little similarity to one another in **Memphis** <note>An ancient capital of Egypt.</note>, **Eleusis** <note>The site of the most famous secret religious rites of ancient Greece.</note>, and on **Samothrace** <note>A Greek island in the northern Aegean Sea, home to the Sanctuary of the Great Gods.</note>.

The Greater Mysteries, on the other hand, had the same content everywhere they were maintained—that is, where they were not taken into exclusive possession by the **Mystagogues** <term>Mystagogues</term><note>From the Greek *mystagogos*; a priest or official who initiated people into the secret rites of the Mysteries.</note>. Regarding those of Egypt, **Clement of Alexandria** <note>A Christian theologian (c. 150–215 AD) who wrote extensively about Greek and Egyptian religious practices.</note> testifies: <margin>\* Clement of Alexandria, *Stromata*, Book 5, p. 566, Paris edition.</margin> "The Egyptians were accustomed to [perform/reveal] their mysteries—

<summary>The author distinguishes between the "Lesser Mysteries," which used regional ceremonies to appeal to the common people, and the "Greater Mysteries," which shared a consistent, secret core across different ancient cultures.</summary>

The futility of the folk-gods and the unity of the supreme being were thus two main doctrines, which the <term>Epopts</term> <note>From the Greek *epoptes*, meaning "eyewitness." This was the highest rank of initiate in the Eleusinian Mysteries, who were permitted to see the sacred objects.</note> or initiates of the Greater Mysteries in Egypt as well as in Greece and Asia propagated among themselves. While the common folk attributed every occurrence incomprehensible to them to a specific supernatural person, those initiates recognized nature as the sole cause of all phenomena, whatever their opinion might otherwise be regarding <term>Jao</term> <note>A Greek transliteration of the Hebrew name for God (Yahweh), often used in mystical or Gnostic texts to denote the supreme deity.</note>, the <term>Demiurge</term> <note>In ancient philosophy, the "craftsman" or creator-god who shaped the physical world.</note>, the primal force of nature, or the essence of all essences. Their god was thus the god of gods, their society was his chosen, priestly people, and their meeting place was his sanctuary.

### V. On the Founding of the Mosaic Religion

...to convert the other half. The entire immense array of measures he had established in his laws against polytheism <term>Vielgötterey: the worship of multiple deities, which Moses sought to replace with the worship of a single God</term> could not prevent any of the unfortunate consequences that the sudden abolition of the old religion and the introduction of a new one was bound to have upon the people. The books that bear his own name <note>The Pentateuch, or the first five books of the Hebrew Bible, traditionally attributed to Moses.</note> are full of examples of the Jews’ protracted vacillation back and forth between superstition and unbelief, of their frequent return to Egyptian idolatry, of their complete forgetting of the new deity, and of their licentiousness, which neither fire nor sword could tame.

However dearly their initiation into the Egyptian mysteries cost the Hebrews, there always remained an essential difference between the religion of the epopts <term>Epopten: from the Greek for "those who have seen"; these were the highest grade of initiates in ancient mystery cults who were supposedly shown the "true" nature of the divine</term> and their own: that what was rational knowledge for the former could never be anything more than blind faith for the latter. And even for this faith, Moses had been able to find no other foundation than the widely prevalent prejudice of those times,

The Hebrews had indeed, for a long time, made no use of this prejudice themselves. Their lowly slave-mentality <note>original: "niedriger Sklavensinn"</note> had suppressed within them the pride that usually kept every other nation faithful to its national god. ### Of the Founding of the Mosaic Religion

nations, [these truths] must have been dangerous; however, as objects of blind faith, they were not only very suitable but absolutely necessary to provide the Hebrews with their state constitution and to secure it for the future. The very same reason that compelled the Egyptian initiates <term>Epopten: derived from the Greek *epoptes*, referring to those who have attained the highest level of initiation in the ancient mysteries, such as those at Eleusis</term> to hide both the vanity of the popular gods and the existence of a single Supreme Being <note>original: "Urwesen"; refers to a primary, uncreated deity from whom all else originates</note> from their common people became, for Moses, the sufficient ground to reveal these secrets to his entire nation. If the laws of the Egyptian land—which were originally built upon the authority of multiple gods—were to retain their former power, then the existence of a God who tolerated no other beside Him had to remain a deep secret from the people. On the other hand, it was precisely this God, and no other besides Him, who had to be announced to the Israelites if a legislation that essentially presupposed belief in a single God was to find acceptance and permanence among them.

In Egypt, the government—which had first been introduced by the gods and handed over to their confidants and favorites—had gradually fallen into secular hands. The

## Section Two.

The multitude and diversity of the gods, and the inseparable discord among the priests that resulted from it, likely contributed the most to this state of affairs. Enough! The royal dignity had separated itself from the priesthood, and as the power of the laity <note>referring to those outside the specialized religious caste</note> spread further and further, ever narrower limits were set upon the influence of the gods and their servants. Secular power finally held all temporal advantages in its hands, while spiritual power retained almost no other support than the shifting religious opinions of the common people <note>original: "Pöbels"; here used to describe the uneducated masses whose beliefs were easily swayed</note>. If the priests did not wish to abandon all their claims, they were forced to drive superstition in the popular religion to its highest extreme. Fortunately for them, the darkness of the mind <note>original: "Finsternisse des Geistes"; a reference to intellectual ignorance or spiritual blindness</note> which they required for this purpose was maintained by the kings themselves, who knew no other way to assert their sovereignty <term>Oberherrlichkeit: the supreme authority or paramountcy of a ruler over their subjects</term> than by seizing, as much as was in their power, all the energies of their subjects. Thus, there were two distinct powers in the state that had to fear one another without being able to restrain one another, and which essentially inflicted the same evils upon the nation; with the only difference being that the secular power, through slav...

### V. On the Foundation of the Mosaic Religion

...ery <note>completing the word "slavery" from the previous page</note> through stupidity, and the spiritual power maintained slavery through stupidity.

To a man of talents and insight, who had been situated for a considerable time as close to the throne as well as the sanctuary of Egypt as Moses had, there could be no lack of opportunities to learn the ill effects of two such powers within a state. It is, of course, difficult to determine which of those ill effects the lawgiver of the Hebrews had primarily in mind when laying the foundation of his new form of government. However, this much at least is visible enough—both in the basic features of his plan and in the spirit of his laws—that none of the cliffs <note>a metaphor for pitfalls or dangers</note> escaped him, upon which the absolute power of priests and <term>mystagogues: those who initiate others into sacred mysteries or religious secrets</term> usually tends to founder. In vain would one seek in the Hebrew constitution for the solution to the great problem: "How the greatest possible freedom of individual men might be reconciled with the greatest advantages of civil society?" <note>This "great problem" was a central theme of Enlightenment political philosophy during the era this text was written.</note> On the other hand, to anyone who has thought through the last four books of Moses <note>referring to Exodus, Leviticus, Numbers, and Deuteronomy in the Old Testament</note> more than superficially, it will...

## ->Second Section<-

...the great hierarchical principle would naturally impose itself: that if the administrators and dispensers of spiritual and invisible goods were not to be disadvantaged compared to those of temporal and visible goods, both dignities <note>original: "Würden"; referring to the status or offices of power</note> had to be united in a single person.

As one comes to know the Israelites from the holy books, they were far less suited than any other nation to accept a system of laws without the intervention of a supernatural power. Jehovah, therefore, had to become their lawgiver in His own person; and if He did not wish to share His political power with any mortal thereafter, He could tolerate no foreign gods beside Him and had to take the government upon Himself. This is exactly what happened, and thus the Hebrews became a "kingdom of priests and a holy nation";\* thereby, the <term>viceroyship of God: the status of acting as God’s earthly representative or deputy</term> was secured for the priesthood for all time. # On the Founding of the Mosaic Religion

...itual beings <note>completing the word "spirits" from the previous page</note> who were called the legislators of other nations, were forced to admit that their confidants and anointed ones <note>original: "Vertrauten und Gesalbten"; referring to those chosen or sanctioned by the divine</note> became subjects of the laity, and the influence of the priesthood decreased in the same proportion as the number of gods increased; for the priests of these various gods, driven by their differing interests, were forced to work against one another. In the Mosaic form of government, however, all these inconveniences were prevented by the fact that **Jehovah** had become all in all: protective deity, lawgiver, and ruler. 

From this point forward, no one could dispute the supremacy of the interpreters of His will <note>referring to the priesthood</note>. All pretensions of men and gods were forever rejected. Infringement upon the sacred prerogatives of the priesthood and the service of a foreign deity had become equally great <term>crimes against the state: "Staatsverbrechen"; in this context, religious dissent was legally equivalent to political treason</term>, which could only be expunged by death. The unity, and consequently the entire strength of political power, rested upon the unity of the object of religion, and both mutually guaranteed their continued existence. —

## Section Three.

### On the Theocracy of the Hebrews.

> What republic could be better or more just than that which confirms God as the ruler of all things, permits the priests to administer the most important matters in common, and appropriately assigns the leadership of the priests to the High Priest? <note>original Latin: "Quae respublica poterit esse melior aut justior, quam ea, quae Deum quidem principem omnium esse confirmat, sacerdotibus autem in communi res praecipuas dispensare permittit, summo vero Pontifici sacerdotum principatum competenter injungit?" — Josephus, *Against Apion*, Book 2. Flavius Josephus was a 1st-century historian whose works are vital for understanding Jewish history under Roman rule.</note>

Jehovah had become, in the truest sense of the word, the King of the Hebrews. He was solemnly installed in the possession of all <term>royal prerogatives: "Majestätsrechte"; these are the specific legal rights, powers, and privileges held by a sovereign monarch</term>. All laws were presented in his name, and the resolutions regarding any doubtful meaning of these laws were sought from him himself. He received an annual tribute <note>The author refers here to the half-shekel tax required of adult males for the upkeep of the Tabernacle/Temple</note> from every man who had reached his twentieth year; he appointed the generals, judges, and magistrates; and without his express command, no war could be started, no battle fought, nor peace concluded, and so forth. In the earliest times, he decided not only the more important affairs of the state, but even condescended to [address] individual... <note>The sentence ends abruptly, continuing on the next page</note>

# On the Theocracy of the Hebrews.

...[could govern]. For through the introduction of the theocracy <term>Theocracy: a form of government where a deity is recognized as the supreme ruling authority, usually administered by a priestly class.</term>, they <note>Referring to the priests, the house of Aaron, and the Levites.</note> had become not only the primary servants but also the immediate representatives of the invisible monarch—the visible governors of Jehovah, to whom he had surrendered all enjoyment and administration of his royal rights.

According to the concepts of that time, Jehovah, as a political king, was the rightful owner of all goods, the land, and even the persons of his subjects. He was content, however (likewise following the custom of the rulers of that time), to have only the best and the firstfruits of all his property delivered to him by the Jews for his actual use. This "use" was nothing other than his granting all those royal revenues to the priests. "Behold (he said to Aaron), I have given you my heave offering <note>original: "Hebopfer"; a specific type of offering in Jewish ritual law that was "heaved" or lifted up as a gesture of presentation to God.</note> of everything that the children of Israel sanctify, for your priestly office, and to your sons as an eternal right. ### Third Section.

legislator; which, however, after the death of Moses, was transferred to the priests and Levites, from whose decisions from then on—just as previously from the decisions of God—no further appeal <note>original: "Appellation"</note> could take place.

The Hebrew hierarchy <term>hierarchy: here referring to the governing body of religious leaders</term> was thus truly in possession of all the advantages and prerogatives <note>original: "Gerechtsamen"</note> which, according to the concepts of that time, were associated with royal dignity. It held the power of Jehovah in its hands, and through this was the visible head of the state; its assistants, administrators, and officials among the laity might be called army commanders, judges, or even kings. As long as the political government of Jehovah lasted (and it lasted, with some intervals excepted, until it was taken over by the Romans), the Hebrew state remained a priestly kingdom despite all the changes that occurred in its external forms of government and in its other fortunes.

# On the Theocracy of the Hebrews.

->I<-

role to play than among any other people in the world. Although it <note>Referring to religious belief or the "popular belief" mentioned on the previous page</note> was indispensable to the Egyptians, Greeks, and Romans for the very first foundation and introduction of political constitutions, it became increasingly dispensable to them in the same proportion as these states—through the progressive increase of their own internal strengths—found the means for their preservation within themselves. Among the Hebrews, on the other hand, it was and remained the sole pillar of the constitution <term>constitution: here meaning the fundamental political and social structure of the nation</term>, the highest sanction of legislation, the only source of sovereign authority, and the only guarantee of all property, and of all rights and claims of both rulers and subjects. Had this belief been suddenly taken from the entire people by a miracle, they would have had to sink in that very moment back into the state in which they left Egypt: into the condition of runaway slaves without morals and without laws, abandoned to the first available impostor who would have placed new shackles upon them in the name of some fetish <note>In the 18th and 19th centuries, "fetish" was a term often used by scholars to describe objects of primitive or superstitious worship</note>—unless the God of their fathers had sent them another Moses.

<summary>This page argues that while other ancient civilizations eventually outgrew their religious foundations, the Hebrew state remained entirely dependent on religious belief as its sole source of law, property rights, and social order.</summary>

### Third Section. On Theocracy etc.

If, therefore, the introduction of the greater mysteries <note>In an 18th-century intellectual context, "mysteries" refers to secret religious or philosophical knowledge reserved for an elite inner circle, distinct from the "popular" religion of the masses</note> among any people was the necessary consequence of the **political indispensability** of **blind faith**, then this seems to have been all the more the case with the **Hebrews**. The **sanctuary** of the **Mosaic religion** <term>Mosaic religion: the system of laws and religious practices established by Moses</term> was at the same time the **cabinet of the state**, from which the government was conducted according to the intentions of the priesthood, and the popular belief had to be steered according to the intentions of the rulers. **Religion** and **politics** here had only one and the same set of secrets, and consequently also a shared key, which had to be in the hands of the heads of state and handed down by them to their successors.

<summary>This page describes the Hebrew sanctuary as the "cabinet of the state," where religious and political power were identical and controlled by an elite class. It argues that because "blind faith" was politically necessary, a system of secret knowledge was required to manage the nation.</summary>

<keywords>Theocracy, Hebrews, Mosaic religion, Priesthood, Political secrets, Sanctuary, Governance, Mysteries</keywords>

## On the Sanhedrin.

> Moses indeed commanded that the common people of the Jews be held bound by all the rituals exactly as they were expressed in the wording of the laws themselves. However, he intended that others, whose minds and virtues were more steadfast, should be liberated from that outer shell, both to become accustomed to a more divine philosophy that is superior to the common mass of men, and to penetrate into the deeper sense of those laws with the eye of the mind. <note>original: "Iudaeorum plebem quidem ritibus omnibus... penetrare voluit." Eusebius of Caesarea was a 4th-century historian; here he is cited to support the idea that the Mosaic Law had a "hidden" intellectual layer for the elite.</note> Eusebius, *Preparation for the Gospel*, Book 7, Chapter 10.

The oldest state constitution of Egypt was theocratic <term>theocratic: a system of government in which priests rule in the name of God or a god</term> for a considerable time, not only in its origin but also in its progression. During this period, the priests were the only visible regents, authorities, and judges. <margin>* Aelianus, *Various History*, Book 14, Chapter 34.</margin> However, they held this power only in their capacity as confidants and authorized representatives of the indig— <note>The German text "einhei-" likely completes to "einheimischen," meaning "indigenous" or "native" rulers.</note>

### Fourth Section.

...indigenous gods. They were thus obliged to spread and maintain opinions among the people that they themselves could not hold as true. If the beliefs the people held regarding their relationship with the gods were to be inherited by their successors—if they intended to leave successors at all—then they could neither keep what they knew of those relationships entirely to themselves, nor share it with everyone indiscriminately; thus, the introduction of a secret and <term>esoteric: intended for or likely to be understood by only a small circle of people with specialized knowledge</term> doctrine for the ruling priests, alongside the public and <term>exoteric: intended for or likely to be understood by the general public</term> one for the people, became unavoidable. The preservation of the popular doctrine depended on the transmission of the secret doctrine, and the Egyptian mysteries became the first example of a sacred state cabinet <note>The author uses "Staatskabinet" to describe a select, secret circle of advisors or ministers who manage the core interests of a government.</note>. Although the Egyptian <term>theocracy: a system of government in which religious authorities rule in the name of a deity</term> had already ceased long before the time of Moses, the mysteries still persisted. While they had lost their former influence over the state, they had not lost the memory or the records of it; and an initiate <note>German: "Eingeweihter"; someone who has been formally admitted into a secret society or religious group.</note> who might have felt called to establish a new theocracy among any other people would have found, in the secret history of those mysteries, the causes of the de- <meta>catchword: de- (completing "Verfall" or "decline" on the next page)</meta>

### Of the Sanhedrin.

...-cline of the priestly rule <note>German: *Priesterregiment*; a system of government where religious authorities hold supreme power.</note>, and to have found the means to prevent it.

In all likelihood, the ancient Egyptian priests had not foreseen that, over time, it would become impossible for them to be everything to their faithful subjects. With the progress of culture <note>In this context, "Kultur" refers to the advancement of civilization and the complexity of social organization.</note> and the gradual increase of societal needs, the necessity first arose to divide the management of civil affairs into several offices, which were not always compatible with the dignity, convenience, and duties of the priesthood. 

In particular, the increasing need to defend the state against hostile attacks had made war the business of a specific class <note>German: *Stand*; referring to a social estate or rank within the hierarchy of society.</note>, which was bound to become as fearsome as it was indispensable to all others. The priests thus saw themselves forced to share their dominion with the man who possessed enough courage and skill to save them and their subjects in the event of encroaching danger, and who, for this purpose, had to command the entire military power of his homeland. The moment must therefore have arrived for Egypt—

# Fourth Section

to emerge, of which **Plutarch** <margin>* On Isis and Osiris. <note>original: *Libro de Iside et Osiri*.</note></margin> has preserved for us the following report: “The kings were chosen either from the priesthood or the military class <note>German: *Soldatenstand*; the social estate comprising professional warriors.</note>, because they believed that the one class should be especially honored for its wisdom, and the other for its bravery. However, if it happened that a soldier was chosen for the government, he was immediately introduced into the **college of the priests** and instructed in their **secret philosophy**, a teaching that, under the guise of fables and allegories, contained various facts and indeed revealed real truths, but at the same time shrouded them under a dark veil of mystery.”

This was thus the way in which the laity <note>German: *Layen*; referring to those outside the priestly order, specifically the military class in this context.</note> were first led into the sanctuary. Their initiation into the mysteries was at once their elevation to the highest state dignities, and at the same moment that the secrets were revealed to them, they became participants in the legislative power. ### About the Sanhedrin

—which the hierarchy should have taken in this dangerous step to ensure their absolute power, only gradually came to light through a series of experiences. The legislator of the Hebrews <note>Referring to Moses.</note> was far too convinced of the necessity of allowing laity <note>German: *Layen*; those not belonging to the priestly class.</note> to participate in the government under certain restrictions not to have taken this into account from the very first design of his new state system. He knew the advantages that the hierarchy could draw from the prestige of the elders among the people, from the heads of the tribes and families, and from the extraordinary talents that would distinguish themselves from time to time among the laity. # Fourth Section.

...step, <note>The text begins mid-sentence, likely continuing a thought about the transition of social classes or the entry into a new state of governance.</note> of which *Plutarch* \* has preserved for us the following report: “The kings were chosen either from the priesthood or the military class, because it was believed that the one class deserved special honor for its wisdom, and the other for its bravery. If it happened, however, that a soldier was chosen for the government, he was immediately introduced into the college of priests and instructed in their secret philosophy—a doctrine that, under the guise of fables and allegories, contained various facts, and revealed actual truths while simultaneously veiling them under a dark shroud of mystery.”

This was, then, the way in which the laity <note>German: *Layen*; individuals who are not members of the clergy or the priestly class.</note> were first led into the sanctuary. Their initiation into the mysteries was simultaneously their elevation to the highest state honors, and at the very moment the secrets were revealed to them, they became participants in the legislative power. These seventy men thus received, through this decree of **Jehovah** <note>A traditional English vocalization of the Tetragrammaton, the four-letter name of God in Hebrew.</note>, a share in the government which they did not previously possess, even as elders and officers of the people. For this purpose, something of the spirit of the lawgiver—that is, of his insights, his purpose, and his plan—was communicated to them. In short, they were given disclosures about things previously unknown to them, and which had to remain secrets for the people forever. They were to be assistants to the ruler, yet in such a way that the burdens rather than the advantages of sovereignty fell to their account: "that they may bear the burden of the people with you." They received only the lowest degree of the <term>greater mysteries</term> <note>In historical and esoteric contexts, "mysteries" refer to secret knowledge or rituals reserved for initiates; the "greater" level implies a deeper understanding of the state's true religious or political foundations.</note>.

# Of the Sanhedrin.

consequently, they were indeed not revealed the complete mystery, but nonetheless a mystery—for: “Since the Spirit rested upon them, they **prophesied** and did not cease.” <note>original: „Da der Geist auf ihnen ruhte, weissagten sie und hörten nicht auf.“ This is a reference to Numbers 11:25.</note>

In all probability, this college of seventy men subsequently lost the key to its secrets, but by no means did it lose its traditional prestige. It maintained itself under the name of the **Sanhedrin** <term>Sanhedrin: The supreme judicial and religious council of the Jewish people, traditionally composed of seventy-one members.</term> or the **Great Council** until the fall of the Jewish state. Its power lasted that long—a power to which even kings had to submit—but so did its dependence on the priests, without whose consent none of its decisions were legally binding. Even in the final days of the nation, King **Herod** <note>Herod the Great (c. 72–4 BCE), the Roman-appointed King of Judea.</note> was accused before this court, and found no other way to escape its jurisdiction and severe punishment than the sudden decision to have the seventy men, and the priest **Aristobulus** <note>Aristobulus III (c. 53–35 BCE), the last of the Hasmonean dynasty and a High Priest, who was seen as a threat to Herod’s power.</note> at their head, strangled at the same time.* Incidentally

## Fourth Section. Of the Sanhedrin.

this assembly had become no less useful for the aims of the Hebrew **hierarchy**, once that portion of the **Mosaic spirit**, which had been bestowed upon them at their founding, had departed from them again. For that reason, the **Sanhedrin** clung all the more firmly to the letter of the law, and exercised its judicial office over all opinions that did not seem to agree with the prescribed creed, with the zeal and unrelenting severity of a Roman **inquisition** <term>Inquisition: The original text uses "Ketzergericht," a court for judging heretics, comparing the Jewish council to the later Catholic Inquisition.</term>. The cause of the **theocracy**, which in early times required that the seventy men should, in part, see things as they were in themselves, had much more to gain later on if these **state inquisitors** walked in blind faith; and it is not improbable that the Sanhedrin owed its later **orthodoxy**, just as it did its former **epoptia** <term>Epoptia: Borrowed from the Greek Eleusinian Mysteries, this refers to the highest stage of initiation where one beholds the "truth" directly rather than through symbols.</term>, to the viceroys of **Jehovah** <note>These "viceroys" refer to the High Priests and leaders who ruled the Jewish state as God's representatives.</note>.

## Of the School of the Prophets.

> The Jewish priests served according to a certain likeness and shadow of heavenly things, explaining in **SECRET** <term>Arcano: Referring to hidden or esoteric knowledge intended only for initiates, as opposed to public law.</term> the meaning of the sacred laws, and what exactly was signified by them. <note>original Latin: "Ad similitudinem et umbram quandam coelestium serviebant sacerdotes Iudaeorum, in ARCANO intellectum sacrorum Legitimorum enarrantes, et quidnam significaretur per ea."</note> — Origen, Book 5, *Against Celsus*.

### Section Five

> "...name of prophecy. Samuel, Nathan, Gad, Ahijah, and Iddo <note>These figures are cited in the Biblical books of Chronicles as authors of records concerning the reigns of Kings David, Solomon, and Rehoboam.</note>, who wrote the history of their times, bear the name of prophets in Holy Scripture for no other reason. — Furthermore, the Hebrew word **Nabi** <note>original Hebrew: נָבִיא (*naví*)</note>, which is translated as 'Prophet' in the **Septuagint** <note>The earliest Greek translation of the Hebrew Bible, created in the 3rd and 2nd centuries BCE.</note>, originally signifies a speaker, or a person who leads the discourse in public assemblies.
