### Section Five.

> “The intention of the historian (says Warburton) <margin>* The Divine Legation of Moses Demonstrated. Book IV. Sect. VI.</margin> in this mention of the school of the prophets was none other than to make us acquainted with the effect which this school had on Saul’s messengers, and on Saul himself. He therefore only casts a sidelong glance at this college <note>The author uses "college" to describe the organized, communal nature of the prophetic school.</note>, and sees and shows it only in the state in which it was found by Saul and his messengers, namely, at prayer. For this reason, only this activity—and not the other occupations of the school of the prophets—is mentioned. They saw two companies <note>original: "zween Chor"</note> of prophets prophesying, or engaged in their devotions. The word prophesying <term>Prophesying (Weissagen): Here understood as ecstatic worship or liturgical singing rather than predicting the future.</term> is explained in this way not only by the Chaldean Paraphrase <note>The "Chaldean Paraphrase" refers to the Targums, which are ancient Aramaic translations and interpretations of the Hebrew Bible.</note>, but also by the apostolic writers, who use it in this very same sense. It can also be assumed with great probability that this religious society was accustomed to beginning and concluding its daily work with a communal praise of Jehovah.”

Warburton introduces several opinions here

# Concerning the School of the Prophets.

### Fifth Section. Of the School, etc.

as it <note>Referring back to the "obedience to the interpreters of Jehovah" mentioned on the previous page.</note> had to be the **main condition** under which a layman was chosen for this throne, he made both the use of this means and the fulfillment of this condition his highest priority. # Fifth Section. On the School, etc.

Since it [submission to the hierarchy] had to be the **main condition** under which a layman was chosen for this throne, he [David] made both the use of this means and the fulfillment of this condition his highest priority. He missed no opportunity to inquire of the priests about the will of **Jehovah**, always kept close to the sanctuary, took refuge in the hierarchy in every danger, was a frequent visitor to the **School of Prophets**, glorified outward worship, etc.; in short, he behaved both before his reign and during it perfectly in accordance with the mind of **Jehovah** and His anointed <note>Referring to the high priests or the lineage of Aaron who were physically anointed with oil.</note>.

### Concerning the Urim.

The holy books of the Hebrews mention the Urim and Thummim rarely, and mostly only in passing, and observe the deepest silence regarding the material and form of these mysterious objects. The relationship of the Urim to

### Sixth Section.

...the office of the High Priest is the only circumstance about which they <note>Referring to the "holy books" mentioned on the previous page.</note> express themselves clearly and definitely. Fortunately, this is precisely the circumstance that must be of the greatest importance to us in our present investigation.

Just as *Jehovah* was not merely a protective deity, but also the political regent of the Hebrews, so his High Priest <term>Oberpriester: literally "upper priest," used here interchangeably with the High Priest (Hohenpriester)</term> had to represent not only the congregation before the altar, but also the throne before the nation. It was, therefore, just as essential to his office to make the King’s will known to the subjects regarding the more important matters of government as it was to present the people's sacrifices and vows to the deity during major festivities and urgent plagues. He was thus required from time to time to obtain *Jehovah’s* commands. *Moses* * was the only prophet in *Israel* whom the Lord had known face to face. After his death, there was a need for a perceptible sign <term>sinnliches Zeichen: a physical or sensory sign that can be perceived by the human senses, rather than a purely spiritual one</term> of the continuing interaction between the invisible King and the

# Sixth Section

Thus, the Urim appears as the most essential state secret, as the main driving force, and as the most powerful means of preserving the theocracy <note>a system of government in which priests rule in the name of God</note>. Nothing can be more evident than the great and certain advantages gained by introducing this oracle for the governors of Jehovah. Through this single means, the most critical matters of state were removed from the arbitrary will of secular state officials and reserved for the King <note>Jehovah, the invisible King of Israel</note> himself, who settled and decided them solely with his first minister <note>the High Priest</note>. The generals and judges—who, precisely because they had received their power from Jehovah, would have been all the more easily able to play the master in Jehovah’s name for their own account—were kept in the strictest dependence on the head of the hierarchy by this means; the prerogatives of the first visible power in the state were secured against all <term>prescription<note>original: "Verjährung," a legal term referring to the loss of a right or claim due to the passage of time</note></term>; and it had become true in the strictest sense of the word: that the army commander of Israel and the entire nation with him were to go out and come in from now on according to the mouth <note>original: "Munde," used here to mean the authoritative command or pronouncement</note> of the High Priest.

<vocab>Urim, state secret, theocracy, oracle, governors of Jehovah, hierarchy, High Priest, Israel</vocab>

# Concerning the Urim and Thummim

Jehovah’s express command—which directed even the supreme commander of His people to the Urim of the High Priest—leaves us with no doubt that all the subsequent, direct divine decisions so frequently encountered in the Books of Joshua and Judges were granted through the Urim. This is true despite the fact that this circumstance is not always explicitly mentioned, presumably because of how commonplace it was.

When the Jewish hierarchy <note>the ruling body of priests and religious leaders</note> subsequently yielded to the impetuosity of the people, who were dissatisfied with Jehovah’s direct rule, and granted the Benjaminite Saul the title of King—with greater power than any previous general had possessed—and Saul was reckless enough to actually exercise this power, the political influence of the Urim naturally fell into jeopardy. The secular <term>Regent<note>original: "Reichsverweser," meaning an administrator or governor of the realm acting on behalf of the true sovereign, in this case, Jehovah</note></term> of the theocracy acted of his own accord—and was rejected by Jehovah. ### Section Six.

“...me Ephod). And when Abiathar had brought the ritual vest <note>original: “Leibrock,” referring here to the Ephod</note> to David, David questioned the Lord and spoke: Shall I pursue these troops, and will I overtake them? He spoke: You shall overtake them and achieve a rescue.” — The success on this occasion once again justified David’s trust in the priest’s Urim, as well as the reliability of this oracle.

The phrase: “Bring the vest to me” <note>original German: “Bringe mir den Rock her”; original Latin: “Applica ad me EPHOD”</note> which David used in both the cited cases, is explained by the custom established by the command of Jehovah, by virtue of which the Urim, with its container, the pectoral <note>the ceremonial breastpiece or breastplate</note>, was never separated from the ephod <note>a sacred linen apron-like garment</note> of the High Priest. The Urim, the pectoral, and the ephod were therefore subsequently used as synonymous expressions whenever the High Priest’s oracle was being discussed. The pectoral, as the seat of the Urim, was also called in Hebrew the equivalent of *divinatio* <note>prediction or divination</note>, and in Latin *Rationale* <note>meaning an object or being endowed with reason/judgment</note>. Even the ephod itself, to which the Urim was attached along with the pectoral, is [referred to] in the Arabi—

# On the Urim and Thummim.

<meta>Ink bleed-through from reverse page visible.</meta>

...the remaining points which we still have on our hearts regarding the **Urim** <term>Urim: traditionally interpreted as "Lights," one of the two objects used by the High Priest to determine God's will</term>, we shall leave to the readers' own reflection; after we have said just a few words about its Egyptian origin, which this oracle shares with several other religious customs of the Hebrews.

The institution of oracles—that reliable channel of political influence and rich source of pure income for the "shepherds of souls" <note>A poetic or cynical term for priests and religious leaders</note> across all ages and nations—was, as is well known, originated in Egypt, just like almost all other inventions of pious policy and offspring of superstition. Speaking idols were among the oldest types of oracles. This is proven at least by their early spread among the Chaldeans, Phoenicians, Syrians, and Arabs, about which we are told

<margin>questions regarding Buxtorf's *History of the Urim and Thummim*, Chapter 2 <note>Johannes Buxtorf the Younger (1599–1664) was a prominent Hebraist who wrote extensively on Jewish antiquities.</note></margin>

> The breastplate <note>original: *Rationale*; the ceremonial breastpiece worn by the High Priest</note> was made square and doubled, intended as a base and bearing images of two virtues: *Manifestation and Truth.* <note>original: *Manifestationem et Veritatem*; these Latin terms were commonly used to translate the Hebrew "Urim" and "Thummim."</note>

<summary>This page argues that the Urim and Thummim, like many other religious practices of the Hebrews, likely originated from Egyptian oracular traditions. It highlights how oracles historically served as tools of political and financial influence for religious leaders.</summary>

### Sixth Section.

...the remnants of ancient historians have preserved so many traces.*

Moses encountered these oracular images during his stay in Egypt, and the Israelites—who, as is well known, had so completely forgotten their ancestral worship <term>vaterländischen Gottesdienst: the traditional religious practices of their patriarchs, like Abraham and Isaac</term> in favor of the Egyptian folk religion—would, by all appearances, have been far less willing to give up these speaking idols than many other pagan customs that **Jehovah**, out of indulgence for the stubbornness and perversity of this people, incorporated into his Law. "The Egyptians had (according to the testimony of Abenephius <note>A name used by Athanasius Kircher for an Arabic source; Abenephius was believed to be an Egyptian priest who converted to Islam, though his identity is debated by modern scholars</note> in Kircher's works...

* <note>original: "Erexerunt (Zabii aut Chaldaei) stellis imagines..."</note> (The Sabians <note>original: *Zabii*; a group of people from Harran often associated with star worship in medieval texts</note> or Chaldeans) erected images to the stars—specifically golden ones to the Sun, and silver ones to the Moon — — Then they built small shrines and placed the images within them, believing that the powers of the stars flowed into those images, and that they possessed the power of understanding, bestowed the gift of prophecy upon men, and finally indicated what things were useful and beneficial to them. *Maimonides, Guide for the Perplexed <note>original: *More-Nevochim*; the influential 12th-century work of Jewish philosophy</note>, Part 3, Chapter 29.* The same *Maimonides* testifies that he had read two entire volumes of the Sabians concerning *these speaking images* and their manufacture. <header>Concerning the Urim and Thummim.</header>

...[Egyptian] figurines,* certain small images shaped like little boys, which in their language were called **Serapes** <note>original: *Serapes*; likely a variation of Serapis, though here described as small oracular figurines rather than the great Greco-Egyptian deity</note>. They were worshiped by them and consulted regarding future and hidden matters. They were set up everywhere in the most prominent places of the cities and honored with burning incense. It was also customary to carry them on one’s person to protect oneself against misfortunes. Of this very same kind were the **Teraphim** <note>original: *Theraphim*; small household idols or cult objects mentioned several times in the Hebrew Bible, notably in the stories of Rachel and Laban</note>, which were revered by the Israelites, and which **Laban**, according to the testimony of the Scripture, had worshiped.” —

“These Teraphim” (writes **Christoph Castro** <note>Christoph de Castro (1551–1627), a Spanish Jesuit theologian known for his commentaries on the Bible</note>, a writer who, as we shall soon see, could have had nothing less in mind than to infringe upon the divinity of the Mosaic customs <note>The author is pointing out that even a strictly orthodox Catholic scholar like Castro acknowledged the physical similarities between pagan and Jewish religious objects</note>.) “These Teraphim were small statues or

---

> \* <note>The following is the original Latin for the passage quoted above.</note> The Egyptians possessed certain images in the form of children, called **SERAPES** in the Egyptian tongue, which they adored while asking about future and hidden things. They were placed throughout the more famous locations of the cities, and people were accustomed to burning incense before them and carrying them with them against evil occurrences. Similar to these were the **TERAPHIM**, which the Israelites worshiped, and which Laban is recorded in the scriptures to have adored.
> *Cited in Kircher, Oedipus Aegyptiacus <note>A famous 17th-century work of Egyptology and comparative religion</note>, Volume I, Treatise 4, Chapter 3.*

### On the Thummim.

The observation that the **Thummim** <note>the name means 'perfections' or 'truth'</note> within the **pectoral** <note>original: *Pektoral*; the breastplate of the High Priest</note> of the **High Priest** with the

> * The **Teraphim** <note>original: *Theraphim*; small household idols</note>, however, were small figures, or idols, which when questioned, gave answers in an audible voice. Since God wished to wean that people <note>the Israelites</note> away, and gently call them back from the customs of the gentiles and the worship of demons—just as He allowed them various types of sacrifices which were previously offered to demons but later presented to God—He likewise preserved for the Israelites those marvelous methods by which the demons made themselves appear wondrous to men for predicting the future and revealing hidden things. However, these oracles, lots, omens, portents, and prophetic visions were now through God Himself and for His honor. Among these are numbered the **Teraphim**, in place of which He gave them the **Urim and Thummim**, but only by means of the **High Priest**, so that the opportunity to worship those idols would be removed from the ignorant common people. They were, therefore, two small, expertly crafted images, made either by the hand of God or by the craftsmen of the ornaments, which the priest carried between the folds of the breastplate <note>original: *rationalis*; the Latin term for the High Priest's Breastplate of Judgment</note>; and whenever something was to be asked, he brought them out. Through them, God, or an angel in God's name, answered clearly and distinctly.
> *Christoph de Castro, Book 3 on Prophecy, Chapter 3.*

### Section Six.

**Urim** were kept together; the fact that these two mysterious things are often mentioned together in the Scriptures, and that nowhere in them is the difference between the two explicitly stated—was, by all appearances, what led the aforementioned writer <note>referring to Christoph de Castro</note> to place both in the same category, and to view the **Thummim** just like the **Urim** as a speaking divine image. However, a somewhat greater attention to the passages of the **Bible** in which the **Urim** and **Thummim** are mentioned could have led him to the opposite conclusion, which **Spencer** <note>John Spencer (1630–1693), an English scholar who wrote extensively on Hebrew rituals</note> has discovered with great probability. Whenever the **Thummim** is named alongside the **Urim**, it only occurs in cases where the items contained in the **pectoral** <note>the High Priest's breastplate</note> are being discussed in general, and without regard to their actual use. On the other hand, as often as the **oracle** of the pectoral or **ephod** <note>a sacred vestment worn by the High Priest</note> is discussed, the **Urim** always appears alone; as is visible in the already cited passages: "He shall ask counsel for him after the judgment of the **Urim**" <note>Numbers 27:21</note>—"The Lord answered him neither by dreams, nor by the **Urim**"—and others.

<summary>This page critiques the theory that the Thummim was a speaking idol like the Urim, arguing instead that the Urim alone was used for seeking oracles while the Thummim served a different purpose.</summary>

### Of the Urim and Thummim.

M

Meanwhile, it is certain that the **Thummim**, if not part of the **oracle** <note>a method of receiving a message from God</note>, at least belonged to the office of the **High Priest**. It could be worn by him alone and was specifically part of his ceremonial vestments, worn upon the breast. This is, admittedly, all that can be gathered from the sacred books of the **Hebrews** regarding the **Thummim**. Nevertheless, even these few details deserve much closer attention because they are established facts. They provide the thread that we may safely follow into the **Egyptian sanctuary** <note>this refers to the scholarly practice of looking for the origins of Hebrew rituals in Egyptian culture, where the Israelites lived for centuries</note>, where so many other Hebrew mysteries have already been clarified, to seek further information about the **Thummim**.

### Section Six: On the Urim, etc.

# Section Seven.

—me a few small remarks, which at least should make this decision easier for some of you. I can and will base these on nothing but facts that have been presented to the public for a few years now, and which are being presented daily in their documents and evidence with ever-increasing completeness.

It is high time, my brothers, that we at least learn the most essential aspects of our **history of the Order**. The insights that we have so long sought in vain within our **sanctuary** <note>original: "Heiligthume"; refers to the inner, secret space or the metaphorical temple of the lodge</note> currently seem to be forcing themselves upon us with all their might from the outside. It would be a very misplaced pride to want to know nothing of the newly discovered, important documents of our history simply because we must borrow them from their current owner—(the public)—who offers them to us for free, without a mysterious air, and without **hieroglyphs** <note>original: "Hieroglyphe"; here referring to the symbolic, coded language of Freemasonry that obscures plain meaning</note>.

I will, of course, have to go beyond the knowledge of our **Master’s degree** <term>the third and final degree of basic "Blue Lodge" Masonry</term>. ->2.<-
### Masonic School of Prophets.

Just as the School of the Prophets stood in a much closer relationship with the greater mysteries of the ancient Hebrews than even the high Sanhedrin <note>The supreme judicial and religious council of the ancient Jewish people</note> and its secular associates; so, my brothers! there is also a Masonic institute which lies much closer to the possessors of the secret art than even the assembly of our Order’s heads—even back in the times when it consisted of defenders of the Temple <note>A reference to the "Strict Observance" branch of Freemasonry, which claimed descent from the Knights Templar</note>. This institute is the actual and true School of the Prophets of Freemasonry. In the same, one learns * reason "as a—"

### Section Seven: Hints toward, etc.

H.. and B...* Not to mention several others. In each of these, one will find the spirit of the successors of Aaron <note>A biblical reference to the high priesthood, used here to describe the high-ranking "Clerical" masons who claimed spiritual authority over the lodges</note> and their guild. This is the same spirit that recently poured itself out in that unctuous pastoral letter,** which so faithfully and emphatically warns the true and genuine Freemasons "against the false shimmer of Illumination <note>A direct reference to the *Illuminatenorden* or Order of the Illuminati</note>, and of Minerval wisdom <term>Minerval wisdom: Referring to the "Minerval" degree, the entry-level rank of the Bavarian Illuminati, named after Minerva, the goddess of wisdom</term>, which brings both temporal and eternal ruin." It is this same spirit, finally, which—not wanting to leave it at mere words—actually inflicted temporal ruin upon the wayward Freemasons in Bavaria <note>This refers to the 1784–1785 suppression of the Illuminati and associated Masonic lodges in Bavaria by Elector Karl Theodor, prompted by the conservative "Clerical" and Rosicrucian factions</note>. This served as a warning example and a wholesome terror to all those who would dare to try to transform the organ of blind faith into an organ of reason.
